<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which option best describes this protein’s function in nucleotide biosynthesis?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> A glutamine-dependent enzyme that uses ATP to convert XMP to GMP in guanine nucleotide biosynthesis.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> An enzyme that uses ATP and bicarbonate to produce carbamoyl phosphate for pyrimidine biosynthesis.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> The glutaminase component that hydrolyzes glutamine to release ammonia for transfer to a partner enzyme.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> The PurL component of FGAM synthase, which uses ATP to transfer glutamine-derived ammonia to FGAR, producing FGAM in purine biosynthesis.<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>Let me analyze this protein sequence systematically.<br><br>**Sequence identification:** The sequence beginning &quot;MKLRYLNILKEKLGREPTFVELQAFSVMWSEHCGYSHT...&quot; is highly similar to E. coli PurL (UniProt P00471), the formamidophosphoribosylaminoimidazole (FGAM) synthetase. The protein is ~450 amino acids long.<br><br>**Domain architecture analysis:**<br>- **N-terminal region (~residues 1-180):** Contains &quot;SEHCGYSHT&quot; which includes the catalytic cysteine of an Ntn-hydrolase (glutaminase-type) fold. This domain can hydrolyze glutamine, though in E. coli this function is primarily carried out by the partner protein PurQ.<br>- **Central region:** Contains the Walker A/P-loop motif &quot;GGATGRD&quot; — a classic ATP-binding motif essential for the synthetase reaction.<br>- **C-terminal region:** Contains the synthetase domain that binds FGAR and catalyzes the formation of FGAM.<br><br>**Evaluating the options:**<br><br>- **Option A (GMP synthetase/GuaA):** GuaA is typically ~515 aa and has a different domain architecture. The sequence does not match GuaA.<br>- **Option B (Carbamoyl phosphate synthetase):** CarB/CarA in E. coli are ~320 aa each and have different motifs. This doesn&#x27;t match.<br>- **Option C (Standalone glutaminase):** While there is a glutaminase-like domain, the protein is too large (~450 aa) for a standalone glutaminase. It clearly contains additional functional domains (ATP-binding Walker A motif, synthetase region).<br>- **Option D (PurL):** This perfectly matches. PurL is the synthetase component of FGAM synthase (step 5 of de novo purine biosynthesis). It uses ATP to convert FGAR to FGAM, receiving ammonia from its partner PurQ (the glutaminase subunit). B. Glucosamine-6-phosphate isomerase - This is a different type of enzyme (not NAD-dependent, doesn&#x27;t use Rossmann fold).

C. UDP-galactose 4-epimerase - This interconverts UDP-glucose and UDP-galactose by epimerization. Key motifs include the Rossmann fold AND the YXXXD motif (YFIDK or similar), which is characteristic of 4-epimerases.

D. UTP-dependent uridylyltransferase (GlcNAc-1-P uridylyltransferase) - This doesn&#x27;t use a Rossmann fold; it&#x27;s a nucleotidyltransferase.

Now, let me look at the specific motifs more carefully:

The sequence contains &quot;YFIDK&quot; which is the YXXXD motif (specifically Y-F-I-D-K). This is the catalytic tetrad motif of UDP-galactose 4-epimerase. In GALE (UDP-galactose 4-epimerase), the catalytic residues include a tyrosine, aspartate, serine, and histidine that are involved in the 4-epimerization mechanism.

The &quot;TGGQG&quot; and &quot;DPKG&quot; motifs confirm NAD-binding via a Rossmann fold.

The &quot;DFLN&quot; motif is also characteristic of the epimerase family.

Looking at &quot;GKGG&quot; - another Rossmann-related motif.

The YXXXD motif (YFIDK here) is the most diagnostic feature. In UDP-galactose 4-epimerase, this motif is highly conserved and forms part of the active site. The tyrosine in this motif acts as the catalytic acid/base for the epimerization reaction.

In UDP-glucose 6-dehydrogenase (option A), the mechanism involves oxidation (NAD+ to NADH with hydride transfer) rather than epimerization, and while it shares the Rossmann fold, it has different catalytic residues and lacks the YXXXD motif in the same configuration.

The presence of the YXXXD motif (YFIDK), combined with the Rossmann fold (TGGQG, DPKG), strongly points to UDP-galactose 4-epimerase.

Let me also check if this matches known GALE sequences. Human GALE is about 348 amino acids. This sequence appears to be around 348 amino acids as well, which fits.

Looking at the sequence more carefully:
- &quot;GGQG&quot; at ~position 100 - Rossmann fold GxGG motif
- &quot;DPKG&quot; at ~position 130 - part of TGxxxDxxG in Rossmann
- &quot;YFIDK&quot; at ~position 200 - YXXXD catalytic motif
- &quot;DFLN&quot; - characteristic of epimerases
- &quot;GGFL&quot; near the end - another conserved region

This is clearly UDP-galactose 4-epimerase (option C).

The question specifically asks to &quot;compare the chemical transformations each assignment entails rather than relying on generic enzyme-like sequence features alone.&quot; 

- Option A involves oxidation (removal of hydride, two-electron oxidation at C6)
- Option C involves epimerization (stereoinversion at C4, which is a hydride transfer to/from NAD+ followed by return of hydride from the opposite face)
- Option B involves isomerization (no cofactor, different mechanism)
- Option D involves transferase reaction (no cofactor, different fold)

The YXXXD motif specifically enables the epimerization mechanism by positioning the catalytic tyrosine to serve as the acid/base for the transient oxidation/reduction of the substrate by NAD+. This distinguishes it from the dehydrogenase (option A) which uses a different set of catalytic residues for net oxidation.

My answer is C.
